Why. OkHttp invokes the Authenticator for every failed response independently. Without synchronization, every thread believes it is the one that must refresh.

Production impact. If the backend rotates refresh tokens (one-time use), the first refresh invalidates the refresh token and the remaining four get 401 on the refresh endpoint — and the usual "401 on refresh = log the user out" logic mass-evicts users. The classic incident: a logout spike after a release with no visible cause.

Senior approach. Serialize refresh behind a Mutex: the first thread refreshes, the rest wait, and after entering the critical section re-check whether someone already refreshed. Plus a retry cap to avoid loops.

class TokenAuthenticator @Inject constructor(
    private val tokenStore: TokenStore,
    private val refreshApi: RefreshApi
) : Authenticator {
    private val mutex = Mutex()

    override fun authenticate(route: Route?, response: Response): Request? {
        if (response.priorCount() >= 2) return null // loop guard
        val failedToken = response.request.header("Authorization")
            ?.removePrefix("Bearer ")

        val newToken = runBlocking {
            mutex.withLock {
                val current = tokenStore.accessToken()
                // Someone refreshed while we were waiting on the mutex
                if (current != null && current != failedToken) current
                else refreshApi.refresh(tokenStore.refreshToken())
                    ?.also { tokenStore.save(it) }
            }
        } ?: return null // refresh failed -> logout flow

        return response.request.newBuilder()
            .header("Authorization", "Bearer $newToken")
            .build()
    }

    private fun Response.priorCount(): Int =
        generateSequence(priorResponse) { it.priorResponse }.count()
}

A common follow-up: why an Authenticator and not an interceptor? Because the Authenticator is invoked only on 401 and already holds the failed response, while an interceptor would have to distinguish statuses and replay requests manually. The interceptor remains responsible for proactively attaching the current token to every request.

2. Flow: catch only handles upstream#

Problem. A developer adds .catch { } and assumes the Flow is now "safe". The crash still happens.

Why. catch intercepts exceptions only from operators above it in the chain (exception transparency). An exception inside collect { } is downstream — it flies past catch and crashes the collector's scope.

Production impact. A crash in render logic (say, NumberFormatException while formatting backend data) kills the coroutine and with it the state subscription. The UI "freezes": state keeps updating but nobody collects anymore.

Senior approach. Keep collect razor-thin (assignment/rendering of a ready model only), move mapping and validation upstream (map/mapNotNull), and model errors as part of UI state rather than as exceptions.

repository.orders()
    .map { it.toUiModel() }          // a failure here -> catch works
    .catch { emit(OrdersUi.Error(it)) }
    .collect { ui ->
        _state.value = ui            // assignment only, nothing to fail
    }

3. StateFlow: equal values never reach collectors#

Problem. The ViewModel emits a "new" state but the UI does not update. Or fast intermediate values are lost.

Why. StateFlow (1) conflates — a slow collector only sees the latest value; (2) compares the new value to the old via equals() and skips emission when they are equal. For a data class with identical fields, copy() produces an equal object — no emission.

Production impact. The typical case is "re-showing" a one-shot event via state: state.copy(showToast = true) does nothing the second time because the value is already true and equal. Or an error snackbar fails to appear for the second identical error.

Senior approach. Separate state and events: state lives in StateFlow (where conflation and deduplication are desirable), one-shot events go through a dedicated effects channel (Channel.receiveAsFlow() or a carefully configured SharedFlow). If "equal" emissions are genuinely semantically distinct, add a discriminator field (timestamp/id) — but that is usually a smell that an event was stuffed into state.

4. SharedFlow: replay, buffer and dropped events#

Problem. An event emitted into a SharedFlow(replay = 0) before any subscriber exists disappears forever. Or tryEmit silently returns false and the event is lost.

Why. A SharedFlow without replay keeps no history; emit with BufferOverflow.SUSPEND and zero buffer suspends until all subscribers accept the value, and in that configuration tryEmit simply fails when a slow subscriber exists.

Production impact. A navigation event sent during Activity recreation (no subscriber for a moment) is dropped — the user taps a button and nothing happens. Nearly impossible to reproduce locally.

Senior approach. For one-shot events use Channel(BUFFERED) + receiveAsFlow(): the channel holds events until a consumer appears and guarantees delivery to exactly one consumer. State the trade-off explicitly: a Channel does not support multiple subscribers, but for navigation/toasts that is precisely the semantics you want.

5. Compose: unstable parameters and excessive recomposition#

Problem. The list stutters; the profiler shows hundreds of recompositions in composables whose data "did not change".

Why. Compose can skip recomposition only when all parameters are stable and equal to the previous ones. List, classes with var, types from modules without compiler metadata are unstable — Compose plays safe and always recomposes. A separate trap: lambdas capturing an unstable object.

Production impact. Scroll jank and extra battery drain on mid-range devices; invisible on the developers' flagship phones.

Senior approach. Read compiler metrics and Layout Inspector recomposition counts instead of guessing. Make UI models immutable (val + kotlinx.collections.immutable, or @Immutable/@Stable with the contract actually honored), hoist lambdas into remember or method references, use a stability configuration file for classes from other modules. And always add: measure first — not every recomposition is a problem.

@Immutable
data class CartItemUi(
    val id: String,
    val title: String,
    val priceFormatted: String
)

@Composable
fun CartRow(item: CartItemUi, onClick: (String) -> Unit) { /* ... */ }
// Call site: onClick = remember { { id -> viewModel.onItemClick(id) } }
// or a method reference: viewModel::onItemClick

6. Fragment: lifecycleScope vs viewLifecycleOwner#

Problem. Collecting in fragment.lifecycleScope updates a view that no longer exists, or subscriptions get duplicated.

Why. A Fragment has two lifecycles: the fragment itself and its view. On the back stack the view is destroyed (onDestroyView) while the fragment survives. A coroutine in lifecycleScope outlives the view and keeps references to destroyed binding/views. Returning from the back stack runs onViewCreated again — adding a second collector.

Production impact. View leaks, crashes on a null binding, doubled handlers (events delivered twice). The single most common Android leak in legacy codebases.

Senior approach. The canonical pattern: viewLifecycleOwner + repeatOnLifecycle(STARTED); binding nulled in onDestroyView. In code review this is the first thing checked in fragments.

override fun onViewCreated(view: View, savedInstanceState: Bundle?) {
    viewLifecycleOwner.lifecycleScope.launch {
        viewLifecycleOwner.repeatOnLifecycle(Lifecycle.State.STARTED) {
            viewModel.state.collect(::render)
        }
    }
}

7. WorkManager: periodic work is not exact scheduling#

Problem. The business asks for "sync exactly every 15 minutes", the developer schedules PeriodicWorkRequest(15, MINUTES) — and sync runs "somewhere between 15 and 40 minutes, and not at all overnight".

Why. 15 minutes is the minimum interval, not a guarantee. The system batches work and defers it for Doze/App Standby buckets, constraints (network, battery) and vendor "optimizers" (some OEMs kill background work far more aggressively than AOSP).

Production impact. Data goes stale unpredictably; sync metrics look great in the office and terrible in the field. The team blames the backend for weeks.

Senior approach. State the rule: WorkManager is for deferrable guaranteed work. For exact timing — AlarmManager's setExactAndAllowWhileIdle (with the SCHEDULE_EXACT_ALARM permission and the awareness that Google restricts it to genuinely time-critical cases — alarms, reminders). For realtime — FCM push as a trigger. And always add sync-on-app-open as compensation: users see fresh data exactly when they look at the screen.

8. Room: destructive migration on user data#

Problem. A fallbackToDestructiveMigration() left over from prototyping ships to production. After a schema bump, part of the user base loses local data.

Why. It was convenient at MVP stage, and nobody wrote migration tests because "the schema will change anyway".

Production impact. For an offline-first app this is loss of unsynced user data: drafts, cart, local edits. That is not a bug — it is lost trust and a one-star review.

Senior approach. Enable exportSchema = true, keep schema JSONs in VCS, write explicit Migrations and test them with MigrationTestHelper against real schemas. A destructive fallback is acceptable only for a pure cache fully recoverable from the network — and that decision is recorded explicitly, not "left as is".

val MIGRATION_5_6 = object : Migration(5, 6) {
    override fun migrate(db: SupportSQLiteDatabase) {
        db.execSQL(
            "ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PENDING'"
        )
        db.execSQL(
            "CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS index_orders_status ON orders(status)"
        )
    }
}

9. Paging 3: refresh, append and prepend are different errors#

Problem. Every paging error is rendered as a full-screen error. A user who scrolled deep into the list on a flaky network loses all already-loaded content from view.

Why. LoadState has three independent components — refresh, append, prepend — while the UI handles only one "generic" error.

Production impact. UX degrades exactly where Paging was supposed to help: on slow networks. Users report "the list disappears".

Senior approach. Map states separately: refresh.Error + empty cache → full-screen error with retry; refresh.Error + cached data → snackbar over content; append.Error → footer with a retry button; with a RemoteMediator, distinguish mediator.refresh from source.refresh (network failed, but the cache is still shown).

10. SavedStateHandle is not persistence#

Problem. Large objects or critical data get stuffed into SavedStateHandle "to survive process death".

Why. Three mechanisms with different guarantees get confused: SavedStateHandle/rememberSaveable (survive recreation and process death, but small data only — the whole state goes through a Binder transaction with a ~1MB limit), in-memory ViewModel (survives only configuration change), persistent storage (survives everything including reboot).

Production impact. TransactionTooLargeException — a crash not in your code but when the system saves state; or a "lost" form because a ViewModel without SavedStateHandle did not survive a background kill.

Senior approach. A three-tier rule: identifiers and small UI state (scroll position, entered text, selected tab) → SavedStateHandle/rememberSaveable; anything reloadable → reload by id; anything the user created and fears losing (a draft) → Room/DataStore at input time, not in onStop.

11. DataStore is not a database#

Problem. Entity lists, history, feed caches end up in Proto/Preferences DataStore — "because it is convenient, one Flow".

Why. DataStore reads and writes the whole file atomically. No partial updates, no queries, no indexes. Every change serializes the entire object.

Production impact. As the file grows, every write gets more expensive, updates contend (updateData is serialized), startup reads slow down. And partial data migration becomes impossible.

Senior approach. DataStore is for settings, tokens, flags, small config objects. Relational/list data belongs in Room. Additionally: always set a corruptionHandler (files do get corrupted) and use SharedPreferencesMigration when moving off legacy prefs.

val settingsStore: DataStore<Settings> = DataStoreFactory.create(
    serializer = SettingsSerializer,
    corruptionHandler = ReplaceFileCorruptionHandler { Settings.getDefaultInstance() },
    migrations = listOf(SharedPreferencesMigration(context, "legacy_prefs") { prefs, current ->
        current.toBuilder().setDarkTheme(prefs.getBoolean("dark", false)).build()
    })
) { context.dataStoreFile("settings.pb") }

12. R8: reflection, serialization and "works in debug, crashes in release"#

Problem. The release build crashes or silently returns null fields where debug works perfectly.

Why. R8 removes "unused" code and renames classes. Anything reached via reflection — Gson, Moshi in reflection mode, custom serialization, JNI, some DI patterns — breaks, because R8 cannot see those accesses. A separate trap: generic signatures that Gson reads from metadata vanish without -keepattributes Signature.

Production impact. The worst case is not a crash but silent nulls in DTOs: business logic runs on garbage data. Play Console crashes are unreadable without the mapping file.

Senior approach. (1) Remove reflection systematically: kotlinx.serialization or Moshi codegen instead of Gson — almost no keep rules needed. (2) Where reflection remains — targeted keep rules on the DTO package, never a global minification opt-out. (3) Test the release build (a smoke suite on the minified build in CI). (4) Upload mapping.txt to Play Console / Crashlytics and archive the mapping for every release.

// proguard-rules.pro (fragment, only if reflection-based serialization remains)
// -keepattributes Signature, *Annotation*
// -keep class com.app.network.dto.** { <fields>; }

13. PendingIntent: mutability as an attack vector#

Problem. A PendingIntent delegates your identity: the receiver executes the Intent on behalf of your app, with your permissions.

Why. A mutable PendingIntent wrapping an implicit intent lets a malicious app fill in the blanks (component, extras) and act in your name — a classic vulnerability. That is why targetSdk 31+ makes the mutability flag mandatory.

Production impact. Either a security hole (mutable where unnecessary), or an IllegalArgumentException crash when targeting a new SDK without an explicit flag, or broken notification actions when IMMUTABLE was set where the system must fill the Intent in (direct reply via RemoteInput requires MUTABLE).

Senior approach. Default — FLAG_IMMUTABLE + an explicit intent (component set). MUTABLE only for documented cases (RemoteInput, some bubbles/geofencing APIs), with the intent narrowed as much as possible. Knows FLAG_UPDATE_CURRENT vs FLAG_CANCEL_CURRENT semantics and that PendingIntents with the same request code/intent (per filterEquals) are the same system object.

val pi = PendingIntent.getActivity(
    context,
    requestCode,
    Intent(context, OrderDetailsActivity::class.java)
        .putExtra("orderId", orderId),
    PendingIntent.FLAG_IMMUTABLE or PendingIntent.FLAG_UPDATE_CURRENT
)

14. Deep links: an incoming URI is untrusted input#

Problem. Deep link parameters are consumed directly: a WebView opens a URL from a parameter, an id is plugged into a request, flags like isPremium=true influence logic.

Why. Anyone can craft a deep link: another app, a malicious site, an email. An exported Activity with an intent-filter is a public API of your app.

Production impact. Arbitrary URLs opened in a WebView carrying session cookies (phishing/token theft), paywall bypass via flags, crashes on garbage parameters, navigation into internal screens around authorization.

Senior approach. Validate every parameter (type, format, allow-list of hosts for URLs), never carry authorization decisions in a URI, gate deep links through the same auth-check layer as internal navigation. For trusted links — App Links with a verified domain (assetlinks.json) rather than a custom scheme any app can hijack. Enable StrictMode.detectUnsafeIntentLaunch in suspicious areas.

fun parseOrderDeepLink(uri: Uri): Screen? {
    val id = uri.getQueryParameter("orderId")
        ?.takeIf { it.matches(Regex("^[0-9]{1,12}$")) }
        ?: return null               // garbage -> home, no crash
    return Screen.OrderDetails(id)   // the navigation layer re-checks auth
}

15. Cancellation: swallowed CancellationException and NonCancellable cleanup#

Problem. A blanket catch (e: Exception) around suspend code also catches CancellationException — the coroutine "refuses to die" and scopes stop cancelling cleanly. The mirror problem: cleanup in finally calls a suspend function and is cut off immediately because the coroutine is already cancelled.

Why. Cancellation in coroutines is cooperative and implemented via an exception. It is a control signal, not an error.

Production impact. Work leaking past screen exit, zombie requests, unclosed resources, flaky tests. Very hard to debug because everything just "sometimes hangs".

Senior approach. Either catch specific exceptions or rethrow CancellationException; for suspend cleanup use withContext(NonCancellable) only for short critical operations (closing a transaction, finishing a log write).

try {
    syncOrders()
} catch (e: CancellationException) {
    throw e                       // never swallow
} catch (e: IOException) {
    _state.value = SyncState.Error(e)
} finally {
    withContext(NonCancellable) { // otherwise a suspend call here dies instantly
        journal.write("sync finished")
    }
}

Follow-up questions by level#

Middle+: how do catch and retry differ in Flow?
Both operate only on upstream exceptions. retry re-subscribes to the upstream a given number of times (with a predicate and backoff via retryWhen); catch handles terminally: after it the upstream is complete, you can at most emit a fallback. Operator order is critical: retry before catch.

Senior: how do you test the "parallel 401s, single refresh" scenario?
MockWebServer: enqueue several 401s, one successful refresh and successful replays; launch N concurrent requests via async and assert exactly one request hit the refresh endpoint (counting recorded requests by path). A separate test: refresh returns 401 and all requests resolve into exactly one logout signal.

Staff/Lead: how do you scale this knowledge to the team instead of keeping it in one head?
Turn the cases into infrastructure: lint rules (ban fragment.lifecycleScope for collect, ban mutable PendingIntent without a justified suppress), shared base classes/conventions (a single TokenAuthenticator, a single LoadState mapper), CI gates (migration tests mandatory on schema change, smoke on the minified build), and ADRs explaining why. Knowledge not encoded into tooling leaves with people.

Android Platform Deep Dive: permissions, storage, notifications, startupexpand / collapse

Platform constraints are a favorite filter for candidates who only ever worked "inside the ViewModel". A Senior must know how Android's evolution broke and reshaped real apps.

Runtime permissions across Android versions#

  • Android 6 (API 23): dangerous permissions became runtime. The request → rationale → request pattern was born.
  • Android 10–11: background location split into a separate permission and flow; scoped storage became the default; one-time permissions ("Only this time"); auto-reset of permissions for unused apps.
  • Android 12: approximate vs precise location (the user may grant only coarse); new Bluetooth permissions (BLUETOOTH_SCAN/CONNECT) replacing location for BLE.
  • Android 13 (API 33): POST_NOTIFICATIONS — notifications are now opt-in; granular media permissions READ_MEDIA_IMAGES/VIDEO/AUDIO replacing READ_EXTERNAL_STORAGE.
  • Android 14 (API 34): partial photo access (READ_MEDIA_VISUAL_USER_SELECTED) — the user can grant access to selected photos only; mandatory foreground service types.

The Senior framing: "a permission is not a dialog, it is a state machine: granted / denied / permanently denied / partial — and it can change while the app is in background, which restarts the process". So permission state is checked at every use, never cached at startup.

Bonus points for saying the best permission is the one you never request: the Photo Picker provides photo selection with zero storage permissions, CREATE_DOCUMENT/SAF saves files without permissions — most media features need no media permission at all.

Scoped storage and file access#

The post-Android 10/11 model: an app freely owns only its app-specific directories (filesDir, getExternalFilesDir — no permissions, removed on uninstall). Shared media goes through MediaStore (your own inserts need no permission; reading others' media needs media permissions; editing others' media needs consent dialogs like createWriteRequest). Arbitrary documents — through the Storage Access Framework with persistable URI permissions. MANAGE_EXTERNAL_STORAGE is effectively banned by Play policy except for file managers.

Production trap: persistable URI permissions are limited in count and disappear on reinstall — document URIs stored in Room must be validated before use, or you get a SecurityException months after the user picked the file.

Notifications: channels, permission, FGS#

Channels (API 26+) are settings in the user's hands: importance, sound and vibration are set at channel creation and from then on owned by the user; you cannot programmatically raise importance. Channel design is therefore a product decision: separate channels for critical (order status) and marketing, so users can kill the latter without losing the former.

Android 13 adds POST_NOTIFICATIONS: the right strategy is to ask in a moment of value (after the first order: "want to track your delivery?"), not on first launch. Denial means a foreground service runs without a visible notification — alive, but invisible to the user.

Foreground services on Android 14 require a declared type (location, dataSync, mediaPlayback…), and some types require extra runtime checks. Starting an FGS from the background is banned since Android 12 (with exemptions: high-priority FCM, exact alarms, user interaction). The Senior conclusion: a "long background process" is designed from the constraints backwards — first ask whether it should be an FGS at all, or WorkManager + expedited work, or a push-driven model.

Task, back stack and deep links: edge cases#

  • singleTask + deep link: if the Activity already exists in the task, the new Intent arrives in onNewIntent and the back stack above it is cleared. A forgotten onNewIntent → the screen "ignores" repeated deep links.
  • Deep link into a deep screen: back must lead to the logical parent, not exit the app. That means a synthetic back stack (TaskStackBuilder / NavDeepLinkBuilder); Compose Navigation builds the stack from the graph itself.
  • launchMode + taskAffinity: combinations with singleInstance/taskAffinity create multiple app tasks in Recents — users see "two apps". The almost-always-right answer: do not touch launchMode without a documented reason.
  • Notification → Activity: the standard mistake is opening a details screen without its parent stack, so back goes home. TaskStackBuilder fixes it.
  • Process death + back stack: the system recreates the back stack, but each Activity gets only its own savedInstanceState — singletons and static "data buses" are empty. Every screen must be able to rebuild itself from an id.

App startup: TTID/TTFD, App Startup, Baseline Profiles#

Terms: cold start (no process) → TTID (time to initial display, first frame) → TTFD (time to full display — the app signals it via reportFullyDrawn(), otherwise Play vitals measure the wrong thing). Warm/hot — the process or Activity already exists.

Typical startup killers: heavy ContentProvider SDK initializers (every SDK loves bootstrapping via a provider), synchronous disk reads in Application.onCreate, reflective config deserialization, eagerly building the whole DI graph. Tooling: Macrobenchmark (StartupTimingMetric) in CI, Perfetto for investigations, Play Vitals for field data.

Fixes in priority order: (1) remove the work from startup entirely (lazy DI, deferred SDK init via the App Startup library with explicit dependencies, or your own lazy mechanism); (2) Baseline Profiles — a profile of critical paths AOT-compiled at install time, removing JIT warm-up: typically −15–30% cold start, generated by a Macrobenchmark module and shipped with the release; (3) startup tracing so you never optimize blind.

// Macrobenchmark: measure cold start + verify the Baseline Profile effect
@Test
fun coldStartup() = benchmarkRule.measureRepeated(
    packageName = "com.app",
    metrics = listOf(StartupTimingMetric()),
    iterations = 5,
    startupMode = StartupMode.COLD,
    compilationMode = CompilationMode.Partial() // with baseline profile
) {
    pressHome()
    startActivityAndWait()
}

Follow-up questions by level#

Middle+: the user denied a permission twice. What happens and what do you do?
The system stops showing the dialog (requests instantly return denied), shouldShowRequestPermissionRationale returns false. The only path left is explaining the value and sending the user to app settings via ACTION_APPLICATION_DETAILS_SETTINGS. The UI must degrade gracefully: the feature is off, the app still works.

Senior: why does your "optimized" cold start in Macrobenchmark not match Play Vitals?
The benchmark measures one device, a fixed state and usually TTID; vitals are a field distribution across all devices with thermal throttling, background load, and medians/percentiles. And if reportFullyDrawn is never called, TTFD in vitals is wrong. So I look at percentiles (P90), segment by device class and calibrate the benchmark on weaker hardware.

Staff/Lead: how do you design a permissions policy at product level?
The minimization principle: prefer permissionless APIs first (Photo Picker, SAF), then contextual asks with rationale at the moment of value, a grant-rate metric per permission in analytics, and an explicit degraded mode per denial. This reduces onboarding drop-off and Play review risk at the same time.

Kotlin/JVM Internals for Android interviewsexpand / collapse

The classic Java question banks did not disappear — they mutated into Kotlin form. Below are modernized versions of what old lists called "equals/hashCode", "generics", "String immutability".

inline, noinline, crossinline, reified#

Why it exists. A lambda on the JVM is an object; in hot paths (collection operators, builders) function-object allocations cost. inline embeds both the function and the lambda body at the call site — zero allocations — and unlocks two unique mechanisms: non-local return from the lambda, and reified type parameters (the type is available at runtime without passing a Class, because the code was inlined with a concrete type).

Modifiers. noinline — when the lambda must be stored/passed on (an object is created after all); crossinline — the lambda will be invoked from another context (another lambda, a Runnable), so non-local return is banned but inlining is kept.

inline fun <reified T> Bundle.parcelableCompat(key: String): T? =
    if (Build.VERSION.SDK_INT >= 33) getParcelable(key, T::class.java)
    else @Suppress("DEPRECATION") getParcelable(key) as? T

Trade-offs for a Senior answer: inlining grows bytecode (matters for large functions), and a public inline function in a library module embeds its body into consumers — changing the body requires recompiling consumers; it is part of the ABI. Inline small functions with lambda parameters, not "everything for speed".

Variance: in, out, star projection#

The expected formula: out T — the type only "produces" T, so List<Dog> can be used as List<Animal> (covariance); in T — the type only "consumes" T, so Comparator<Animal> fits where Comparator<Dog> is expected (contravariance); * — "some unknown type": read as Any?, write nothing concrete.

sealed interface UiEvent
class EventBus<in T : UiEvent> { fun send(event: T) {} }      // consumer
interface DataSource<out T> { suspend fun load(): List<T> }   // producer

val analytics: EventBus<UiEvent> = EventBus()
val clicksBus: EventBus<ClickEvent> = analytics // ok: in-variance

The Android tie-in that lifts the answer to Senior: Flow<out T> is covariant — so Flow<LoadedState> substitutes for Flow<UiState>; Comparable and Continuation<in T> are stdlib examples. Use-site variance (fun copy(from: Array<out Item>)) is for invariant classes used by a specific function as producer/consumer only.

equals/hashCode and data class pitfalls#

  • Arrays in data classes: Array.equals is referential. Two data classes with identical array content are unequal → DiffUtil/StateFlow treat them as "different", the UI flickers. Fix: List instead of Array, or manual equals/hashCode with contentEquals.
  • Mutable fields + HashSet/HashMap: put an object in a set, mutate a field — the hashCode changes and the object "vanishes" from the set while still being in it. Keys and set elements must be immutable.
  • Fields outside the constructor: equals/hashCode/copy include only primary-constructor properties — a property declared in the body is ignored, which surprises in comparisons.
  • copy() breaking invariants: a private constructor with validation is bypassed by the public copy. If invariants are critical — a regular class with manual equals, or a factory.
  • The Compose/StateFlow link: all deduplication (StateFlow conflation, recomposition skipping) stands on equals — a broken equals means either redundant updates or missed ones.

Java interoperability: where it hurts#

  • Platform types (String!): values from un-annotated Java skip compiler null checks — the NPE arrives at runtime in an unexpected place. Practice: pin the type explicitly at the Java boundary (val name: String?) and normalize.
  • Checked exceptions: Kotlin does not declare them; Java callers are not forced to catch. For a public API consumed from Java — @Throws.
  • Default parameters: Java sees only the full signature; @JvmOverloads generates overloads (the classic: custom Views with three constructors).
  • companion object: from Java it is Companion.foo() unless @JvmStatic; constants — @JvmField or const val.
  • SAM conversion works for Java interfaces and Kotlin fun interface — the main glue in legacy codebases.
  • Coroutines are not callable from Java directly (suspend has a hidden Continuation parameter) — expose callbacks/CompletableFuture or Flow→LiveData adapters at the boundary.

JVM vs ART: what to say#

The old "Dalvik vs ART" question now sounds like this: Kotlin compiles to JVM bytecode, D8 converts it to DEX, R8 layers shrinking/obfuscation/optimization on top. ART executes DEX via a mix of interpretation, JIT and profile-guided AOT: hot paths get compiled per profile (local plus cloud profiles from Play). Baseline Profiles are how you "seed" that profile at install time. This connects runtime internals to the practical question of startup performance — exactly the bridge expected from a Senior.

Follow-up questions by level#

Middle+: why can't reified exist without inline?
Type erasure: in compiled code the type parameter is erased and does not exist at runtime. Inline copies the function body to the call site where the concrete type is known to the compiler, so T::class is substituted statically. Without inlining you would pass Class<T> by hand — which is exactly what Java does.

Senior: value classes — when and why?
Type safety without allocations: @JvmInline value class UserId(val raw: String) compiles to the underlying String in most positions, but the compiler will not let you confuse a UserId with an OrderId. Pitfalls: boxing when used as nullable, generic or behind an interface; name mangling for Java interop. I use them for domain identifiers and units (Money, Percent) — cheap protection against a whole class of bugs.

Staff/Lead: the team argues about inline functions in a shared core module. Your decision framework?
Weigh the gain (lambda allocations on hot paths, reified convenience) against the cost (binary compatibility — the body is embedded into consumers, changes force recompilation; DEX growth). Fine for small utilities in a monorepo built together; risky for an independently versioned published artifact. Either way the decision goes into an ADR with criteria, not verbal folklore.

Networking and Data: Senior deep diveexpand / collapse

This section deepens the base Networking/Persistence chapters: this is what gets asked right after the words "now imagine production".

OkHttp: interceptors vs authenticators#

Division of responsibilities: an application interceptor handles cross-cutting logic before the cache (headers, domain-level request logging, short-circuited responses); a network interceptor sees real network traffic after the cache and redirects (compression, low-level logging); an Authenticator is reactive authorization, invoked only on 401, with the failed response in hand. Proactive (attach a fresh token to every request) — interceptor; reactive (recover from 401) — Authenticator. Distinguishing these roles is the marker of someone who has actually configured a client.

A taxonomy of network errors#

A Senior does not return "Exception" from a repository. Errors are classified by what should be done about them:

sealed interface NetworkError {
    data object NoConnection : NetworkError          // auto-retry when network returns
    data object Timeout : NetworkError               // retry with backoff
    data class Http(val code: Int, val body: ApiError?) : NetworkError
    // 401 -> refresh/logout, 403 -> "no access" UI, 409 -> version conflict,
    // 422 -> field validation, 429 -> backoff per Retry-After, 5xx -> retry + alert
    data class Serialization(val cause: Throwable) : NetworkError // broken contract: log + non-fatal
}

The key thought: the retry policy depends on the error class and on the idempotency of the operation. GET — always retryable; a POST "create order" — only with an idempotency key.

Idempotency in payment and order flows#

The trap scenario: a "pay" request went out, the response was lost (timeout). The state is unknown: money may have been charged. Retry without protection = double charge; no retry = a "stuck" payment.

The solution: the client generates an Idempotency-Key (UUID) before the first attempt, stores it with the local operation record (Room, before sending — the outbox pattern), and every replay carries the same key; the server deduplicates. After a timeout the right move is not "create again" but "query the operation status by key". In the interview, stress that this is a contract with the backend: without server-side support the key does nothing.

Paging 3: RemoteMediator and cache invalidation#

The offline-first feed architecture: the PagingSource reads only from Room (single source of truth); the RemoteMediator fetches pages from the network and writes them to Room in a transaction together with remote keys (cursors/next-page tokens). Any change to Room data automatically invalidates the PagingSource — the UI updates by itself.

Senior nuances: (1) REFRESH in the mediator decides whether to clear the table (instant display of the old cache versus a content "jump"); (2) remote keys are stored transactionally with the data, otherwise pagination drifts after process death; (3) cachedIn(viewModelScope) is mandatory — without it PagingData does not survive resubscription and breaks after rotation; (4) jumping/placeholders are a separate decision with UX trade-offs.

Room: transactions, relations, indexes#

  • Transactions: @Transaction for atomic multi-table operations (clear + insert on sync; reading an entity with relations consistently). Suspend DAO methods inside a transaction must not switch dispatchers mid-way — the transaction is bound to its thread/context.
  • Relations: @Relation/@Embedded are convenient for read models, but under the hood they are extra N+1-like queries (Room runs them in a transaction). For hot lists an explicit JOIN into a custom POJO is often more honest.
  • Indexes: every foreign key and every column in WHERE/ORDER BY of frequent queries is an @Index candidate. Verify with EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN and Database Inspector. Trade-off: indexes slow writes — for tables mass-rewritten on sync, index deliberately.
  • Flow from Room: invalidation is table-level — the Flow re-emits even when your specific rows did not change; distinctUntilChanged() after mapping is the standard move.

DataStore: corruption, migration, boundaries#

Recapping the edge-case bank briefly: a corruption handler is mandatory (disks are not perfect), migration off SharedPreferences goes through SharedPreferencesMigration as one atomic step, and large/list data belongs in Room. The extra Senior nuance: updateData serializes concurrent updates — a consistency guarantee and a bottleneck at once, so DataStore is no place for counters updated from several threads dozens of times per second.

Security: tokens, Keystore, pinning#

  • Where to keep tokens: the base level — the app's private storage (DataStore): other apps cannot read it without root. Hardening — encryption with an Android Keystore key: the key is non-extractable from secure hardware, data encrypted with AES/GCM. The honest Senior comment: this protects against offline extraction and backups, not against an attacker with root on an unlocked device.
  • EncryptedSharedPreferences: the Jetpack Security library (security-crypto) is deprecated and unmaintained — new projects are better off with a thin custom Keystore + DataStore wrapper; old ones need a migration plan, not blind tutorial copying.
  • Certificate pinning: protects against compromised CAs and MITM with a user-installed certificate. The trade-offs you must name: certificate rotation becomes a release risk (a pinned cert expires → the app goes blind, and a Play update takes days), so pin the public key (SPKI), keep backup pins, and have a remote-config kill switch. For many cases the alternative is Certificate Transparency monitoring, or pinning only critical endpoints (payments).
  • Also worth mentioning: android:allowBackup and backup rules for sensitive data, a logging-redaction interceptor so PII/tokens never hit logs, and obfuscation ≠ security.

Follow-up questions by level#

Middle+: how does OkHttp's offline cache differ from a Room cache?
The OkHttp HTTP cache is transport-level: driven by Cache-Control/ETag headers, transparent to your code, but offers no queries over data and no control of structure. Room is an application-level source of truth: your own queries, transactions, relations, an explicit freshness policy. For UI data it is almost always Room; the HTTP cache is a cheap bonus for static resources.

Senior: how do you design retry so you don't create a retry storm?
Exponential backoff with jitter (randomization so clients do not hammer in sync), an attempt cap, respect for Retry-After on 429/503, retry of idempotent operations only, a circuit-breaker-like "backend is down — stop hitting it" state in the repository, and a retry-rate metric in observability so you see the storm before it takes the backend down.

Staff/Lead: the backend team proposes moving to GraphQL. Your questions?
What hurts today (over-fetching? versioning?), how caching changes (Apollo's normalized cache versus Room — who is the source of truth?), offline scenarios and pagination, the error contract (GraphQL returns 200 with errors — the whole error taxonomy gets rewritten), battery/payload costs, tooling and codegen in CI, team learning curve. The decision: a pilot on one non-critical screen with before/after metrics, not a big bang.

Release Engineering: R8, signing, Gradle, multi-moduleexpand / collapse

The area a Middle has usually "seen from afar" and a Senior is obliged to own: how code becomes a Play Store release and does not break along the way.

R8: shrinking, obfuscation, optimization, mapping#

R8 does four things: tree shaking (removes unreachable code, with keep rules as roots), obfuscation (short names → smaller DEX; it is not security), optimization (inlining, null-check removal, class merging), resource shrinking (together with AGP). The effective rules are assembled from your proguard-rules.pro, libraries' consumer rules and AGP defaults — so "where did this keep rule come from" is also a debugging skill (-printconfiguration).

Mandatory production practices: archive mapping.txt for every release (Play Console + Crashlytics — otherwise stack traces are unreadable), run a CI smoke on the minified build, and write targeted keep rules instead of -dontobfuscate/-dontoptimize "because something crashed". Typical breakages are covered in edge case #12.

Signing, versioning, staged rollout#

  • Play App Signing: Google holds the signing key; you sign the AAB with an upload key. The benefit — an upload key can be reset if compromised; the app signing key cannot (it is the app's identity forever). AAB + Play generates device-optimized split APKs.
  • Versioning: versionCode — a monotonic int (often derived from CI build number or date), versionName — human semver. In multi-flavor builds — a scheme guaranteeing uniqueness across tracks.
  • Staged rollout: release to 1% → 5% → 20% → 50% → 100% with gates on crash-free rate and key vitals; a rollout can be halted, but installed versions cannot be rolled back — so risky changes hide behind a feature flag (kill switch) that can be flipped without a release. The in-app updates API forces critical updates.
  • Pre-release check: release notes drafted, mapping uploaded, DB migrations tested, new functionality flags default-off, the monitoring dashboard open for the duration of the rollout.

Gradle: build speed and organization#

Build time is team money. Levers by impact: modularization (incrementality and parallelism — Gradle rebuilds only changed modules and their dependents); configuration cache and build cache (local + remote for CI and the whole team); KSP over KAPT (KAPT generates Java stubs — slow; Room/Hilt/Moshi are on KSP already); version catalogs (libs.versions.toml) as the single source of versions; convention plugins (build-logic) instead of copy-pasting config across 60 modules; minimal logic in build.gradle; profiling via build scans.

Variants and flavors: buildTypes (debug/release — how we build) × productFlavors (what we build: brand, environment, market). The scaling trap: combinatorial variant explosion multiplies configuration time and the test matrix; environment is often more honestly kept in runtime config than in a flavor.

Multi-module architecture: dependency direction#

The working skeleton: app (assembly, DI graph, navigation) → feature:* (screens, ViewModels) → core:* (domain, data, network, database, designsystem, common). Rules that keep the graph healthy:

  • feature modules never depend on each other — shared code sinks into core, or communication goes through api interfaces (the feature:x:api + feature:x:impl pattern: only api modules may be depended on);
  • implementation by default, api only when a type genuinely leaks into a public signature: honest contracts and fewer cascading recompilations;
  • navigation between features goes through an abstraction (a Navigator interface in core, implemented in app), otherwise "secret" dependencies appear;
  • the health metric is the graph's depth and width: the build scan shows the critical path of the build.

Dynamic Feature Modules are a separate decision with a narrow niche: large rarely-used features (AR, multi-MB SDKs). The price: SplitInstallManager flows (request, progress, errors, instant restart), harder testing and reflection/interface-based navigation. If the goal is merely code organization, regular modules suffice.

CI/CD, feature flags, observability#

  • Pipeline: PR → static checks (lint, detekt, custom lint rules) → unit tests → build → instrumented/screenshot tests (Gradle Managed Devices or a device farm) → minified smoke → internal track. Releases run as a separate tagged pipeline publishing via the Play Publisher API.
  • Feature flags: three different tools that must not blur together: release toggles (code rollout, deleted after 100%), experiments (A/B with analytics and sticky bucketing), kill switches (operational safety). Hygiene: default = off and safe, defined offline behavior (last cached value), tests for both states of critical flags, regular cleanup of dead flags.
  • Observability: crashes are not enough — you need non-fatals with context, breadcrumbs (navigation, key actions — so a crash has a story), metrics (crash-free users, ANR rate, startup P90, network error rate per endpoint), structured logging with PII off. Alerts on the post-release delta, not on absolute values.
  • Analytics architecture: typed events instead of strings (sealed classes / schema-generated), a single AnalyticsTracker facade (vendors swappable), event schema validation in CI, an event plan agreed with product as an artifact. The most expensive analytics bug is silent: an event stops flowing and nobody notices until the quarterly report — so monitor event volumes too.

Follow-up questions by level#

Middle+: how does AAB differ from APK and why does it exist?
AAB is a publishing format, not an installation one: Play generates split APKs for the specific device (ABI, density, language) — users download less. Signing is then done by Play App Signing. Locally, bundletool tests split behavior.

Senior: 11 PM, after rolling out to 20% crash-free dropped from 99.8% to 97%. Your actions?
First — halt the rollout (stops new installs; free and reversible). Then: Crashlytics — the top new crash, deobfuscate via mapping, identify the segment (OS version? device? feature?). If the crash is in a flagged new feature — flip the kill switch, the rollout can resume. If it is in the core — a hotfix release with priority rollout. In parallel — an incident channel, a timeline, and a postmortem: why didn't the internal track / 1% stage catch it.

Staff/Lead: project build time grew to 25 minutes, the team is angry. The plan?
Data first: build scans, configuration vs execution split, the module graph's critical path, CI cache hit rate. Typical wins ordered by cost: remote build cache, configuration cache, KAPT→KSP, splitting "hub" modules on the critical path, moving environment out of flavors, auditing api→implementation. Then set a budget (e.g., local incremental ≤ 2 min, CI PR ≤ 12 min) and a trend dashboard — otherwise it all comes back within six months.

Android System Design: practical scenariosexpand / collapse

A companion to the framework chapter: ready-made walkthroughs of typical scenarios. Each follows: requirements → architecture and data → offline/errors → security → testing and observability → trade-offs and traps. In the interview do not recite everything — start by clarifying requirements and walk the interviewer through the layers.

1. Offline-first shopping with a cart#

Clarify: can items be added offline? What happens to prices/stock on sync? One device or several (cart shared with web)?

Architecture: Room is the source of truth for the catalog (TTL cache) and the cart (local mutations). Cart mutations use the outbox pattern: the operation is written to a pending_operations table in the same transaction as the optimistic cart update; WorkManager with NetworkType.CONNECTED drains the queue with retry/backoff.

Conflicts: server-authoritative for prices and stock: the sync response overwrites local prices; an "item disappeared" conflict marks the element unavailable and the UI shows a diff ("2 items changed price"). Operations are idempotent with client-generated operation ids.

Errors/offline: the UI always renders local state plus a sync-state banner (synced / pending N / error). Checkout is online-only, with an idempotency key.

Testing: unit tests on conflict-merge logic; an integration test of the outbox (offline operations → network appears → queue drains, order preserved); an E2E happy-path checkout.

Traps: optimistic UI without a visible pending state (users cannot tell what is unsynced); a server-side cart wipe that overwrites local pending operations; endless retries of non-idempotent operations.

2. Food delivery: courier tracking#

Clarify: position update frequency? ETA accuracy? tracking when the app is backgrounded/killed?

Transport: active screen — WebSocket/gRPC stream or SSE (position every 2–5 s); background — FCM push on status changes (not coordinates!) plus a notification; returning to the screen — reconnect plus a state snapshot via REST (the stream carries deltas, REST the full state: the classic snapshot + updates pair).

Architecture: a TrackingRepository exposes StateFlow<DeliveryState>; under the hood it merges the REST snapshot with stream updates and owns reconnect with exponential backoff + jitter. The UI animates the marker by interpolating between points (60 fps rendering from 0.3 fps data).

Lifecycle nuance: the stream lives only while the screen is visible (repeatOnLifecycle / WhileSubscribed(5000)) — otherwise you burn battery and sockets. Ongoing notifications / Live Updates cover status outside the app.

Errors: a staleness indicator when no updates arrive for N seconds; degradation to polling when the WebSocket cannot be established (proxies/firewalls).

Traps: tracking via a persistent location FGS in the customer's app (unnecessary — the server provides coordinates); computing ETA on the client; no message versioning on the stream (out-of-order updates make the marker jump backwards — you need a monotonic sequence number).

3. Chat with offline messages#

Clarify: delivery guarantees? ordering? E2E encryption? media?

Data: Room: messages (client_id UUID — primary until acknowledged, server_id afterwards; status: pending/sent/delivered/read/failed), conversations, outbox. Sending: insert pending into Room (UI shows instantly) → WorkManager/sync engine sends → server returns server_id → status updates. Server deduplicates by client_id.

Inbound sync: push (FCM) as a "wake-up call"; data is pulled incrementally by cursor/sequence per conversation — never rely on the push payload (it may not arrive, or arrive dataless). Ordering — server sequence; the UI sorts by it, not by local time.

Errors: failed messages with manual retry; the post-reconnect "retry storm" is damped by a queue with bounded concurrency.

Security: with E2E — keys in the Keystore and the separate complexity of multi-device; the minimum — TLS + pinning on critical endpoints; the local DB optionally SQLCipher (trade-off: performance and maintenance).

Traps: sorting by client timestamp (clocks lie); no client_id → duplicate messages on retry; full conversation reloads instead of incremental cursor sync; edit/delete left undesigned (they are events, not just state).

4. A news feed with Paging 3 and cache#

Skeleton: Room + RemoteMediator (details in the Networking and Data chapter). Here — the product decisions: pull-to-refresh clears the table in the same transaction that inserts page one; "new posts above" — an "N new posts" banner, never a sudden scroll jump; element keys are stable ids so DiffUtil/LazyColumn do not flicker.

Cache invalidation: a per-page TTL (timestamp in remote keys) — stale cache is shown immediately but triggers a background refresh (stale-while-revalidate). A personalized feed is not cached long — agree with product on the acceptable content "age".

Traps: one error behavior for all LoadStates (edge case #9); losing scroll position after process death (persist an anchor key); images without dimensions from the backend → layout jumps (aspect ratio belongs in the API contract).

5. A payment flow#

Principles: payment state is a state machine (idle → validating → processing → requires_3ds → success/failed/unknown) with the server as the source of truth. No raw card data in the app: tokenization through the provider SDK (Adyen/Stripe) — both PCI DSS scope and less code.

The key case — unknown outcome: a timeout/process death during processing → on return, query the status by idempotency/payment id; the Pay button is blocked idempotently, double-tap is guarded at the state-machine level, not via isEnabled.

3DS/redirect: returning from the browser/bank app via a deep link — validated, with zero trust in its parameters (the server confirms the status anyway).

Testing: unit tests on the state machine (all transitions, including unknown); integration with the provider sandbox; the mandatory "process killed mid-payment" test.

Traps: declaring success from the client SDK response without server confirmation (webhook/poll); logging PANs/tokens; retrying POSTs without an idempotency key; keeping payment state only in ViewModel memory.

6. Authentication and token refresh system#

Components: a TokenStore (DataStore + Keystore encryption), a TokenAuthenticator (single refresh behind a Mutex — code in the edge-case bank), an AuthInterceptor (attaches the header), a SessionManager (StateFlow<SessionState>: LoggedIn/LoggedOut/Expired — navigation subscribes to it).

Scenarios to cover out loud: simultaneous 401s (one refresh); refresh returning 401 → exactly one logout signal, not a storm; logout wipes tokens, the user database, cancels WorkManager jobs and invalidates in-memory caches; multi-account — separate data namespaces per account id.

Security: refresh token rotation (the server issues a new refresh token on every refresh — a stolen old one becomes a compromise marker); a biometric gate as a local layer on top (BiometricPrompt + CryptoObject for strict binding).

Traps: a race between in-flight requests and logout (requests must be cancellable via a session scope); tokens in plain SharedPreferences with allowBackup=true; a deadlock when the refresh call itself passes through the Authenticator (the refresh client is a separate OkHttp instance without an Authenticator).

7. An analytics SDK / module#

Clarify: delivery guarantees? event volume? offline? privacy/consent?

Architecture: a public track(event: AnalyticsEvent) facade (typed events); instant non-blocking write into a Room queue (its own scope with a SupervisorJob — the SDK never crashes the host app); batched dispatch (by size/time/network state) via WorkManager; at-least-once plus server-side deduplication by event id; backpressure: a queue cap with a drop-oldest-low-priority policy.

Data quality: a versioned event schema, CI validation (types generated from the schema), a consent state gating collection (GDPR), PII sanitization at the entry point.

Observability of the SDK itself: queue size, age of the oldest event, delivery success rate — otherwise data loss is silent.

Traps: SDK initialization hitting cold start (lazy init, no ContentProvider magic); events lost when a crash precedes the queue write (queueing is the first step, not the last); unbounded queue growth offline.

8. A feature flag system#

Architecture: a FeatureFlags.isEnabled(flag: Flag): Boolean interface plus a typed flag registry with in-code defaults; sources by priority: local override (debug panel) → remote config → default. Fetch at startup plus periodically; activation of fetched values is controlled (next cold start for UX-critical flags, immediate for kill switches).

Consistency: a flag's value is pinned for the session (otherwise the UI flips mid-flow); A/B buckets are sticky per user id; exposure is logged to analytics at first use, not at startup.

Traps: offline first launch (defaults only — they must be safe); a flag gating the flag-update mechanism itself; dead flags nobody deletes (process: the removal ticket is created together with the flag); testing only the "on" state.

9. A large multi-module app#

This question is not about a list of modules but about rules. The graph skeleton and principles live in the Release Engineering chapter; at system-design level add: ownership (every module has an owning team, CODEOWNERS), contracts between features via api modules, automated graph enforcement (convention plugins forbid illegal dependencies), a shared-UI strategy (a design system module with its own release cycle), and the scaling path: a monorepo with a build cache long before separate repositories.

Traps: a core:common module turning into a dumping ground and a recompilation hub; dependency cycles through "temporary" hacks; premature multi-module in a three-person team.

10. Migrating from XML Views to Compose#

Strategy: strangler fig, not big bang. Order: (1) the foundation — a Compose design system (tokens, base components) plus interop wrappers; (2) new screens — Compose only; (3) old screens by "change frequency × simplicity" (a screen nobody touches migrates last or never); (4) ComposeView in XML and AndroidView in Compose as bridges.

The precondition everyone skips: state management must be ready — if logic lives in Fragments, first refactor to ViewModel + StateFlow (it pays off regardless), and then the UI layer changes almost mechanically.

Quality control: screenshot tests before/after each migrated screen; performance metrics (lists especially: RecyclerView → LazyColumn — verify jank and model stability); APK size and build time monitored (Compose adds to both).

Traps: picking "the most complex screen" as the pilot; interop sandwiches (XML→Compose→XML) — focus, scrolling and theming hurt there; an endless parallel refactor without an explicit definition of done.

11. Migrating from RxJava to Coroutines/Flow#

Concept mapping: Single/Maybe → suspend fun; Completable → suspend fun (Unit); Observable/Flowable → Flow (backpressure via suspension, naturally); Subjects → StateFlow/SharedFlow/Channel per semantics; CompositeDisposable → structured concurrency (scopes instead of manual subscription bookkeeping); Schedulers → injected Dispatchers.

Order: along module/layer boundaries, data first: repositories move to suspend/Flow, with kotlinx-coroutines-rx3 adapters (asFlow/asObservable) as a temporary bridge for old consumers; new features are Rx-free from day one; complex combinator chains (combineLatest pyramids) migrate last — rewritten against behavior tests written BEFORE the migration.

Watch closely: error semantics (Rx onError is terminal; in Flow it is catch/retry plus the CancellationException nuances), hot Subjects with replay (pick the matching SharedFlow replay/buffer), backpressure strategies (onBackpressureLatest → conflate, etc.), tests (TestScheduler → runTest/virtual time + Turbine).

Traps: mechanical operator-for-operator translation without redesign (half of Rx chains emulate what coroutines express as plain sequential code); a permanent dual stack (the goal and the deadline for removing the rx dependency must be explicit); lost unsubscription where CompositeDisposable was cleared manually and the chosen scope is wrong.

How to present scenarios in the interview#

The formula: 2 minutes on requirements and constraints (offline? scale? security?), 5 on the layers (UI state → domain → data → transport) with an explicit source of truth, then volunteer 2–3 failure modes and how observability catches them. The interviewer grades the order of thinking, not completeness: requirements → data → errors → trade-offs. The strongest Senior signal is "here I would check with product…" and "this depends on the backend contract; I would propose…".

Senior in a team: code review, mentoring, technical decisionsexpand / collapse

At Senior/Principal interviews roughly 30% of the signal is not code. "Tell me about a hard technical decision" or "how do you review code" tests whether you scale beyond your own commits.

Code review: what a Senior looks at#

Priority order: (1) correctness and lifecycle safety (scopes, cancellation, leaks, the edge cases from this guide); (2) contracts and API design (is it easy to misuse?); (3) tests — not "do they exist" but "would they catch a regression"; (4) performance on hot paths; (5) style last and ideally automated (ktlint/detekt) so review is about substance. Culture rules: comment on the code, not the person; offer an alternative with every criticism; distinguish "blocker" / "should" / "nit"; ask questions instead of issuing orders where the author may have had a reason. A systemic signal from reviews (the same mistake across people) is converted into a lint rule or a document, not into endlessly repeated comments.

Mentoring#

Working tools worth naming with your own examples: pair programming on the hard parts instead of "rewrote it over the junior's head"; stretch assignments delegated with clear context and definition of done; review as teaching (explaining "why", linking internal ADRs); tech talks / guilds after incidents; and the measurable outcome of mentoring — a person who became autonomous in a domain rather than dependent on the mentor.

Technical decisions and communicating trade-offs#

A frame that lands well in interviews: state the problem in product/money terms → 2–3 options with each option's honest downsides (including "do nothing") → selection criteria (risk, cost, reversibility) → the decision plus a verification plan (metrics, a pilot) → an ADR so the "why" survives a year. Irreversible decisions deserve more debate; reversible ones deserve a quick experiment. Prepare 2–3 real STAR stories featuring a conflict of opinions and a measured outcome: "I argued against rewriting to X; we did Y instead, build time −40%" sounds Senior; "we used Clean Architecture" does not.

Follow-up questions by level#

Senior: your PR got criticized and you disagree. What do you do?
First genuinely understand the argument (ask back), separate taste from substance; if it is substance — thank and fix; if taste — propose codifying a team convention instead of fighting in every PR; if deadlocked — a 10-minute call and a third opinion / the code owner. Blocking a release over a stylistic disagreement is an anti-pattern on both sides.

Staff/Lead: product pushes a deadline; you can see it will hurt without a refactor. How do you communicate?
Translate technical debt into risk and velocity: "without X every next feature in this module costs +30% time — here are the last two as evidence". Offer priced options (shortcut now + a ticket with a deadline; partial refactor of the critical path; full one later), give a recommendation and leave the call to the business — in writing. If the risk is unacceptable (security, data loss) — escalate explicitly; that too is a Senior's duty.

How to prepare for a Senior Android Interviewexpand / collapse

Four-week preparation plan#

Week 1: Android fundamentals and Kotlin#

Review Activity/Fragment lifecycle, process death, permissions, background execution, Kotlin nullability, sealed classes, immutability, generics, and collections.

Week 2: Coroutines, Flow, and Compose#

Focus on structured concurrency, cancellation, exception handling, launch vs async, Flow operators, StateFlow/SharedFlow, recomposition, state hoisting, and side effects.

Week 3: Architecture, DI, networking, persistence#

Practice explaining MVVM/MVI, Clean Architecture trade-offs, repository/source of truth, modularization, Hilt scopes, token refresh, caching, Room, DataStore, and migrations.

Week 4: Testing, performance, and system design#

Prepare stories about tests you wrote, bugs you fixed, performance problems you diagnosed, and systems you designed. Practice chat/offline/payment/feed designs aloud.

Topics candidates often fail#

  • Coroutine cancellation and exception propagation.
  • coroutineScope vs supervisorScope.
  • StateFlow vs SharedFlow and one-shot events.
  • Compose recomposition and side effects.
  • Fragment view lifecycle.
  • Process death vs configuration change.
  • Token refresh edge cases.
  • Modularization trade-offs.
  • Real test strategy instead of tool listing.

How to answer system design questions#

Start with requirements. Define data model and source of truth. Explain sync/networking. Explain UI state and error handling. Discuss lifecycle and background work. Add testing, performance, observability, and trade-offs.

Questions to ask the interviewer#

  • How is the Android app modularized today?
  • What are the main performance or reliability problems?
  • How does the team handle architecture decisions?
  • What is the testing strategy?
  • How are releases, monitoring, and crash triage organized?
  • What would success look like in the first 3–6 months?

Final checklist#

  • I can explain lifecycle, process death, and ViewModel boundaries.
  • I can design cancellation-safe coroutine code.
  • I can model UI state and one-shot events correctly.
  • I can explain Compose recomposition and side effects.
  • I can discuss Clean Architecture without dogmatism.
  • I can design offline-first data flow.
  • I can test ViewModels, Flow, coroutines, and Compose UI.
  • I can reason about performance with measurement tools.
  • I can answer system design questions with trade-offs.
